[^key-647e479acbacec20710bbb56fc0ff33c][^key-e7163d93f4927bf5e6dc2a00abcc9024][^key-367e78afdd47b7a0fecbdba3b6de6571][^key-79876f9213f739977ebd744236aa0d53][^key-e991473321a847685051e45e010b2664][^key-071998b5585335531fb8fa8b8c563410][^key-e422acfa0952882997cfbc7963986b27][^key-963cb93b73defc838a5740ab6137b2af][^key-42c949891d9250c18264808b474c469c][^key-ade2547d22400bdf86b5d0ba9f37022e][^key-84c4bf2dd39c47692f329b91399fee04][^key-b3911d3437bc3b214928a0d925844898][^key-92dc2b9f353c68040fdc6adb69188acb][^key-ab20f2b87d6d8798dcb4b4e1dea79a19][^key-aed710a71440891e3ac6fb1bf040cade][^key-8c2a1ce03137b0f8945981f1179fe903][^key-31f9919b6ab493024135cec36b1da0ad][^key-0eb2103372e80d3f0df376af79e2d3cd][^key-c6bbca971346b1f27bc1686151fe803d]

### PART 11: Witnesses

<table>
<tbody>
<tr><th><strong>11.1</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What is this Part about?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">This Part is about the citation of witnesses and their attendance at hearings.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">This Part is also about measures that the court can take to assist vulnerable witnesses in giving evidence.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>The citation of witnesses</em></td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>11.2</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can a party arrange the attendance of witnesses at a hearing?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">Parties must send each other and the court a List of Witnesses Form at least 2 weeks before the hearing.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">The List of Witnesses Form must set out the witnesses that they want to appear at a hearing.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">A party only needs to cite a witness to appear at a hearing if the party is unable otherwise to arrange for that witness to appear.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">A witness may be cited to appear at a hearing by formally serving on that witness a Witness Citation Notice.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">The Witness Citation Notice must be formally served on the witness at least 3 weeks before the hearing.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>11.3</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What if a witness does not appear at a hearing?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">If a witness is cited to appear at a hearing, the witness must appear at that hearing.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If a witness who has been cited does not appear at a hearing, the sheriff may order the witness to be brought to court.</td></tr>
<tr><td>. . .</td><td colspan="2">. . .</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Vulnerable witnesses</em></td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>11.4</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How will the court treat a child witness?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">If a party cites (or intends to arrange the attendance of) a child as a witness, that party must send the court and the other party a Child Witness Notice.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">A Child Witness Notice asks the sheriff to authorise the use of a special measure in taking the child witness's evidence, or to decide that the child witness is to give evidence without the benefit of any special measure.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">Before the sheriff decides how to deal with the Child Witness Notice, the sheriff may order the parties to provide further information.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may decide to make the orders requested in the Child Witness Notice with or without ordering a discussion in court.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">Where the sheriff decides to have a discussion, the sheriff clerk must send the parties notice of when it will be held.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">At the discussion, the sheriff must consider the Child Witness Notice and decide whether to authorise the use of a special measure in taking the child witness's evidence, or that the child witness is to give evidence without the benefit of any special measure.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>11.5</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How will the court treat other vulnerable witnesses?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">If a party cites (or intends to arrange the attendance of) a witness who is not a child, but the party thinks that the witness is a vulnerable witness, that party may send the court and the other party a Vulnerable Witness Application.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">A Vulnerable Witness Application asks the sheriff to decide whether the witness is a vulnerable witness. If the sheriff agrees, the sheriff may authorise the use of a special measure in taking the vulnerable witness's evidence.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">Before the sheriff decides how to deal with the Vulnerable Witness Application, the sheriff may order the parties to provide further information.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may decide to make the orders requested in the Vulnerable Witness Application with or without a discussion in court.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">Where the sheriff decides to have a discussion, the sheriff clerk must send the parties notice of when it will be held.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">At the discussion, the sheriff must consider the Vulnerable Witness Application and decide whether the witness is a vulnerable witness. If the sheriff agrees, the sheriff may authorise the use of a special measure in taking the vulnerable witness's evidence.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>11.6</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What are special measures?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">Special measures are ways of taking the evidence of a child witness or a vulnerable witness.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may authorise the use of any of these special measures:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>allowing that witness to give evidence before an independent person,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>allowing that witness to give evidence by live television link,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>allowing that witness to use a screen while giving evidence,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(d)</td><td>allowing that witness to be supported by someone while giving evidence.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>11.7</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can a party ask the court to review the arrangements for a child witness or a vulnerable witness?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The party who sent a Child Witness Notice or Vulnerable Witness Application to the court may ask the sheriff to review the arrangements for the child witness or vulnerable witness to give evidence by sending the court and the other party a Special Measures Review Application.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">A Special Measures Review Application asks the sheriff to vary or revoke the current arrangements for the child witness or vulnerable witness to give evidence.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">When a Special Measures Review Application is received, the sheriff may ...:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>vary a special measure,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>add a new special measure,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>substitute a new special measure for an existing one,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(d)</td><td>delete a special measure, or</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(e)</td><td>revoke the order authorising the use of special measures entirely.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">Before the sheriff decides how to deal with the Special Measures Review Application, the sheriff may order the parties to provide further information.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may decide to make the orders requested in the Special Measures Review Application with or without a discussion in court.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">Where the sheriff decides to have a discussion, the sheriff clerk must send the parties notice of when it will be held.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(7)</td><td colspan="2">At the discussion, the sheriff must consider the Special Measures Review Application and decide whether to vary or revoke the current arrangements for the child witness or vulnerable witness to give evidence.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>11.8</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What happens when evidence is to be given before an independent person?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">Where the sheriff authorises a child witness or a vulnerable witness to give evidence before an independent person, the hearing at which the evidence is taken is to be video recorded.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">A party may be present when a child witness or vulnerable witness gives evidence before an independent person only if the sheriff has given permission for this to happen.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">The independent person must send the video recording and any relevant documents from the hearing to the sheriff clerk.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff clerk must send the parties a notice indicating that the video recording has been received.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">If any relevant documents or other evidence are also received, the sheriff clerk must send the parties notice of what they are and when they were received.</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>

[^key-71c7aa41b4d0ac93fa03e9889d1c8146][^key-71c7aa41b4d0ac93fa03e9889d1c8146][^key-e5042a35c78281bd13a6208fddeb6f84][^key-1efb075dbb98654782be415478ed294a]

### PART 12: The hearing

| **12.1** | **What is this Part about?** |
| --- | --- |
| (1) | This Part is about the hearing at which the dispute between the parties should be resolved. |
| **12.2** | **What is the purpose of the hearing?** |
| (1) | The purpose of the hearing is to help the sheriff to resolve the dispute between the parties. |
| **12.3** | **How will the dispute between the parties be resolved?** |
| (1) | The sheriff may refer parties to alternative dispute resolution at a hearing. |
| (2) | If the sheriff thinks a negotiated settlement is possible, the sheriff must help the parties to negotiate a settlement to the dispute. |
| (3) | If no negotiated settlement is possible, the sheriff must resolve the dispute by deciding it at that hearing. |
| (4) | The sheriff may continue the hearing to another day without resolving the dispute only if it is necessary to do so. |
| (5)[^key-ca5dc07dde8b8ad44eca51c1306edfa9] | But the sheriff must not continue a hearing to another day solely because a witness did not appear. |
| **12.4** | **What will the sheriff do at the hearing?** |
| (1) | The sheriff must ask the parties about their attitudes to negotiation and alternative dispute resolution. |
| (2) | The sheriff must identify the factual basis and legal basis of the claim and the response to the claim. |
| (3) | The sheriff must identify the factual and legal matters genuinely in dispute between the parties. |
| (4) | The sheriff must take a note of the hearing. This note is for the sheriff's own purposes and must be kept until any appeal is no longer possible or until any appeal has been concluded. |
| **12.5** | **What if a party does not come to the hearing?** |
| (1) | If the claimant does not come to the hearing or is not represented at the hearing, the sheriff may dismiss the claim. |
| (2) | If the respondent does not come to the hearing or is not represented at the hearing, the sheriff may make a decision in the case at that hearing. |
| (3) | If neither party comes to the hearing and neither party is represented at the hearing, the sheriff must dismiss the claim. |
| **12.6** | **How will evidence be given at the hearing?** |
| (1) | Before evidence is heard, the sheriff must explain to the parties the way the sheriff has decided to consider evidence at the hearing. |
| (2) | The sheriff may impose conditions on how evidence is presented or dealt with, including conditions on how witnesses are questioned or setting time limits on how long witnesses may be questioned. |
| (3) | The sheriff may decide whether the evidence of a witness is to be taken on oath or affirmation or not. |
| (4) | The sheriff may ask questions to the parties or to witnesses. |
| (5) | The sheriff may inspect any evidence with the parties or their representatives present. |
| (6) | The sheriff may inspect any place with the parties or their representatives present. |

### PART 13: The decision

<table>
<tbody>
<tr><th><strong>13.1</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What is this Part about?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">This Part is about the decisions which the sheriff can make to resolve a dispute.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">This Part is also about the circumstances in which a party can apply to have a decision recalled.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>13.2</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>When must the sheriff make the decision?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">At the end of the hearing, the sheriff may either make a decision there and then, or may take time to consider before making a decision.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff takes time to consider a decision, the decision must be made within 4 weeks from the date of the hearing.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>13.3</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How will the sheriff make the decision?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff makes a decision there and then, the sheriff must explain the reasons for that decision to the parties in person.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff takes time to consider a decision, the sheriff must prepare a note of the reasons for the decision, and the sheriff clerk must send that note to the parties.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">In every case, the sheriff must set out the decision in the case in a Decision Form.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may correct any errors in a Decision Form ....</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">The party in possession of a Decision Form which is to be corrected must return that original Decision Form to the court before the corrected Decision Form is sent to that party.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">The Decision Form must be sent to all parties to the action.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>13.4</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What sort of decisions can the sheriff make?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may make any decision which resolves the dispute between the parties, including a decision which:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>orders the respondent to pay the claimant a sum of money,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>orders the respondent to deliver something to the claimant,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>orders the respondent to do something for the claimant,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(d)</td><td>dismisses the claim (or part of the claim) made by the claimant,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(e)</td><td>absolves the respondent of the claim (or part of the claim) made by the claimant.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">A decision which absolves the respondent in a claim means that the claimant cannot make a claim about the same subject against the respondent again.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Recalling a decision</em></td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>13.5</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>When can a decision of the sheriff be recalled?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">A party may apply to have a decision of the sheriff recalled where the sheriff either dismissed a claim in that party’s absence or made a decision in that party’s absence.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff dismissed the claim, a party may only apply for recall within 2 weeks of the claim being dismissed.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff made a decision (other than dismissal) in the case, a party may apply for recall at any time before the decision of the sheriff has been fully implemented.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">A party may only apply to have a decision of the sheriff recalled in a case once.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>13.6</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can a party apply to have a decision of the sheriff recalled?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">A party may apply to have a decision of the sheriff recalled by completing an Application to Recall and sending it to the court.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff made a decision following an Application for a Decision and the respondent wants to dispute the claim or part of the claim, the respondent must include a completed Response Form with the Application to Recall.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff clerk will check whether the Application to Recall is the first Application to Recall in the case by the party making the application.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">If it is the first Application to Recall by that party, the sheriff must send the parties an order arranging a discussion in court at which the sheriff will consider whether to recall the decision.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">The party making the application must send a copy of the Application to Recall and any Response Form to the other party at least 5 days before the date of the discussion in court.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>13.7</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What happens when a sheriff decides to recall a decision?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff recalls a decision then the sheriff must give each party orders setting out the next steps they are to take to allow the dispute to be resolved.</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>

[^key-01c9457610462792beef84dfc0221fe6][^key-5bf3475d60aa944d585ab867ff161b55][^key-377b8309f68e19b2a60f7154ac3d59b4][^key-0bd81cbeba9921e860ea2d3c039a3e52][^key-e9997427b38e156c7b4ae7562864737e]

### PART 14: Expenses

<table>
<tbody>
<tr><th><strong>14.1</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What is this Part about?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">This Part is about the expenses of a claim which the sheriff can order a party to pay for.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>14.2</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What orders about expenses can the sheriff make?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">Once a claim has been resolved, the sheriff must make an order about expenses, such as:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>that no payments are to be made in respect of the expenses of any party,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>that a payment is to be made to a party or to a party's solicitor.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">Expenses incurred by a party to do with a courtroom supporter may not be part of an order about expenses.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>14.3</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>When will the sheriff make an order about expenses?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">In a case where the expenses of a claim are capped, the sheriff must make an order about expenses when deciding the claim.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">In any other case, the sheriff must, if able to, make an order about expenses when deciding the claim.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">If not able to make an order about expenses when deciding the claim, the sheriff may make an order about expenses after deciding the claim.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>14.4</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What if the sheriff does not make an order about expenses when deciding the claim?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff makes an order about expenses after deciding the claim, then the sheriff must not set out the final decision in a case in a Decision Form until the order about expenses is made.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff does not make an order about expenses when deciding the claim, the sheriff must give the parties written orders.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">Those orders must arrange an expenses hearing.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">Those orders must require a party to send an account of expenses to the court and to each other before the expenses hearing.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">Those orders may then require the sheriff clerk to assess the level of expenses (if any) that should be awarded to a party and to send notice of that assessment to the parties before the expenses hearing.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>14.5</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What is an expenses hearing?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The purpose of an expenses hearing is to assess the level of expenses (if any) that should be awarded to a party.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">At the expenses hearing, the sheriff must make an order about expenses, such as:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>that no payments are to be made in respect of the expenses of any party,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>that a payment is to be made to a party or to a party's solicitor.</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>

[^key-4cff84e5370f2c0b05290a7e3d0dbde5]

### PART 15: How to enforce a decision

<table>
<tbody>
<tr><th><strong>15.1</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What is this Part about?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">This Part is about the steps which a successful party must take to enforce a decision.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>15.2</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>When can a party enforce a decision?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">After the Decision Form is sent, a party must wait 4 weeks before enforcing a decision.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">A party must not enforce a decision if that decision is being appealed (see Part 16).</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">A party who is sent an order arranging a discussion in court at which the sheriff will consider an Application to Recall must not enforce a decision until the sheriff has decided whether to recall the decision.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">A party must not enforce a decision which has been recalled.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>15.3</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can a party enforce a decision?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">If a party uses a trading name, a decision which names the party using that trading name may be enforced against the party by that name.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">Before enforcing a decision for the payment of a sum of money in the following ways:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>an earnings arrestment (which is where a deduction is made from the earnings of the other party as a way of paying that sum of money),</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>an attachment (which is where certain goods owned by the other party are seized and sold as a way of paying that sum of money),</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>a money attachment (which is where money in the possession of the other party is seized as a way of paying that sum of money),</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2">the successful party must formally serve a Charge on the other party by sheriff officer using a method of formal service mentioned in rule 18.3.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">The purpose of formally serving the Charge is to give the other party one last chance to pay the sum of money ordered by the court.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">The Charge must demand payment:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>within 2 weeks if the other party is in the United Kingdom,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>within 4 weeks if the other party is outside the United Kingdom,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>within 4 weeks if the other party's address is unknown.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">If the demand in the Charge is not complied with, then the successful party may instruct a sheriff officer to enforce the decision.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">The requirement to formally serve by sheriff officer is subject to the exceptions in sections 2 and 3 of the Execution of Diligence (Scotland) Act 1926.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(7)</td><td colspan="2">The Charge must be in the form set out in the schedule of the Act of Sederunt (Form of charge for payment) 1988.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(8)</td><td colspan="2">Where the Charge is formally served, the sheriff officer is not required to send a Confirmation of Formal Service to the court.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>15.4</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What if the claimant does not know the respondent's address?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">Where the claimant is successful but does not know the respondent's address, the claimant must take all reasonable steps to find out the respondent's address.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If the claimant does not know the respondent's address, then instead of formally serving the Charge on the respondent, the claimant must formally serve it by sheriff officer on the sheriff clerk in the sheriff court district where the respondent's last known address was.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff clerk must then publicise the Charge by advertising its details on the Scottish Courts and Tribunals Service website for 4 weeks.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">After that 4 weeks, the sheriff clerk must certify on the Charge that the advertisement took place and send it to the person who formally served it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">The claimant may then instruct a sheriff officer to enforce the decision.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>15.5</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What if the respondent does not comply with a decision?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">A claimant may make an Alternative Decision Application where the respondent does not comply with a decision which:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>orders the respondent to deliver something to the claimant, or</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>orders the respondent to do something for the claimant.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">An Alternative Decision Application may only be made where the sheriff alternatively ordered the respondent to pay the claimant a sum of money.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">The application is made by sending an Alternative Decision Application to the court.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">After considering the Alternative Decision Application, ...the sheriff may ...:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>grant the application, and order the respondent to pay the claimant a sum of money,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>refuse the application,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>order the claimant to appear at a discussion in court, where the sheriff will consider whether to make any orders.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">A copy of the alternative Decision Form is to be sent to all parties.</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>

[^key-b8762b99fd2ce9dbf5c8d939ebafef91][^key-b73e3ca49bf147e8305065bf274716bb][^key-3b933b72961706be49e563fb3119f433][^key-f9c0db2f38bb79f59565fcf549e25256][^key-b26ce8fcb4b05eb5879dc213d6ff6ce1][^key-7c01cf53293c2898bbc2791fde37b629][^key-ce3b830b703617b30abebcc1197619c1][^key-42abc5a349783cd2250dd09de3ab9a28][^key-d7bba6aa29fbae4437acc0accb569ac8][^key-85a7cce60ed29c42824b8a3d42fd9df0][^key-1702b4e71e83240a67c270b1b909aa4e]

### PART 16: How to appeal a decision[^key-cf8b955e07a2736139d58e0b955f4614]

<table>
<tbody>
<tr><th><strong>16.1</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What is this Part about?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">This Part is about how a party can appeal a decision and how the sheriff and Sheriff Appeal Court will deal with an appeal.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>16.2</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How do you appeal a decision?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">A party may appeal a decision within 4 weeks from the Decision Form being sent.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">A party may appeal a decision by sending a completed Appeal Form to the sheriff court.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">That party must at the same time send a copy of the completed Appeal Form to the other party.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">The Appeal Form must set out the legal points which the party making the appeal wants the Sheriff Appeal Court to answer.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">A party may not appeal a decision if that party can apply to have that decision recalled (see Part 13).</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>16.3</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What will the sheriff do with an appeal?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff must prepare a draft Appeal Report within 4 weeks of the court receiving an Appeal Form.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">The draft Appeal Report must set out the factual and legal basis for the decision which the sheriff came to.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">The draft Appeal Report must set out legal questions for the Sheriff Appeal Court to answer.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff clerk must send the draft Appeal Report to all parties.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">All parties may, within 2 weeks of the draft Appeal Report being sent to them, send the sheriff a note of any other legal points they wish the Sheriff Appeal Court to answer and any factual points in the draft Appeal Report they disagree with.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may order a discussion in court to consider whether amendments should be made to the Appeal Report.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(7)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may then amend the Appeal Report.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(8)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff must then sign or authenticate electronically the Appeal Report.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(9)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff clerk must send a copy of the signed Appeal Report to each party.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(10)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff clerk must transmit the following to the Clerk of the Sheriff Appeal Court:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the note of the reasons for the sheriff's decision (if one was prepared),</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>a copy of the Decision Form,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>all written orders,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(d)</td><td>the signed Appeal Report, and</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(e)</td><td>any note sent to the court by a party.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>16.4</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What will the Sheriff Appeal Court do with an appeal?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The Clerk of the Sheriff Appeal Court must, within 2 weeks of receiving the signed Appeal Report, arrange an appeal hearing and send all parties notice of where and when the appeal hearing is to be held.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">Unless the Sheriff Appeal Court orders otherwise, an appeal hearing must be before one Appeal Sheriff.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">At the end of the appeal hearing, the Sheriff Appeal Court may either make a decision there and then, or may take time to consider the decision.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">If the Sheriff Appeal Court takes time to consider the decision, the decision must be made within 4 weeks from the date of the appeal hearing.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4A)</td><td colspan="2">The President of the Sheriff Appeal Court may vary the period mentioned in paragraph (4).</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">If the Sheriff Appeal Court makes a decision there and then, it must explain the reasons for that decision to the parties in person.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">If the Sheriff Appeal Court takes time to consider a decision, the court must prepare a note of the reasons for the decision, and the Clerk of the Sheriff Appeal Court must send that note to the parties.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(7)</td><td colspan="2">The Sheriff Appeal Court may alter the decision which the sheriff made by either amending the Decision Form or issuing a new Decision Form.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(8)</td><td colspan="2">Parts 2, 4, 5 and 6 of the Act of Sederunt (Sheriff Appeal Court Rules) 2021 apply to the appeal.</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>

[^key-eedcb20422ffd84a97a2ba8967f0c997][^key-7005498dafcc2c641b1612368a8daf09][^key-07add1d48361d616391661141ea5f5a1][^key-7b3f48a2f06e016e8b91a1e80695ec24]

### PART 17: Miscellaneous matters

<table>
<tbody>
<tr><th><strong>17.1</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What is this Part about?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">This Part is about some miscellaneous matters which can arise during a case.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.2</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can a case be transferred out of the simple procedure?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">Where a sheriff orders that a case should no longer proceed subject to these rules, that order must identify the procedure under which the case is to continue.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff orders that the case should proceed as an ordinary cause, the sheriff must also order three things:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>that the claimant must lodge an initial writ and intimate it to every other party within 2 weeks from the date of the order,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>that the respondent must lodge defences within 4 weeks from the date of the order, and</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>that an options hearing is to be held on the first suitable court day occurring not sooner than 10 weeks (or such lesser period as the sheriff considers appropriate) after the last date for lodging the initial writ.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff orders that the case should proceed as an ordinary cause the sheriff may also certify in the order that the importance or difficulty of the proceedings makes it appropriate to transfer the case to the Sheriff Personal Injury Court.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.3</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can the sheriff make a reference to the Court of Justice of the European Union?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">If a question of EU law arises in a case, the sheriff may refer that question to the Court of Justice of the European Union using the CJEU Reference Form.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may decide to do this when asked to by a party, or without being asked.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff must draft the reference within 4 weeks of deciding to do so.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">Once a reference has been drafted, the sheriff clerk must send a copy to the parties.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">Once the draft reference has been sent to the parties, each party has 4 weeks to send suggested amendments of that reference to the sheriff.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">Once that 4 weeks has passed, the sheriff has 2 weeks to consider any suggested amendments.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(7)</td><td colspan="2">At the end of that period of 2 weeks, the sheriff must finalise and sign the reference.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(8)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff clerk must transmit the reference to the Court of Justice of the European Union and inform parties that the reference has been made.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.4</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can a statutory intervener intervene?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">A statutory intervener may apply to the sheriff to intervene in a case by sending to the court and to the parties an Application to Intervene.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">The Application to Intervene must set out the reasons for the proposed intervention, the issues which the intervention would address, and the reasons why the intervention would assist the sheriff.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may grant the application with or without a discussion, but there must be a discussion if a party asks for one.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may grant the Application to Intervene only if satisfied that:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the case has a relevant connection to one of the functions of the statutory intervener,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>the intervention is likely to assist the sheriff, and</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>the intervention will not unduly delay or otherwise prejudice the interests of the parties, including their liability for expenses.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may impose conditions on the intervention.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may invite the statutory intervener to intervene in a simple procedure case by sending to the statutory intervener and to all parties an Invitation to Intervene.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(7)</td><td colspan="2">An Invitation to Intervene must be accompanied by a copy of the Claim Form and the Response Form, and any other documents relevant to the reasons for the proposed intervention.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(8)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may impose conditions on an intervention when making an invitation.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.5</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What can a statutory intervener do in an intervention?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">An intervention is a written submission of 5,000 words or less (including any appendices).</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">A copy of the intervention must be sent to all parties.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">In exceptional circumstances, the sheriff may allow a longer written submission or an oral submission.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Management of damages</em></td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.6</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>When is a damages management order available?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">Damages management orders are available:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>where a claimant who is under a legal disability asks for the payment of a sum of money as damages,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>where another person makes a claim on behalf of a person who is under a legal disability asking for the payment of a sum of money as damages.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">In either case, a damages management order is only available if the person who is under a legal disability is 16 years of age or older.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.7</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>When must the sheriff make a damages management order?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff must make a damages management order if the sheriff orders the respondent to pay the claimant a sum of money as damages.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff must also make a damages management order if the claimant accepts an offer from the respondent to pay a sum of money as damages to settle the claim.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.8</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What can the sheriff do in a damages management order?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff must make an order about how the money is to be paid to and managed for the person under a legal disability.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may order the money to be paid to different people to be managed for the benefit of the person under a legal disability.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may order the money to be paid to:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the Accountant of Court,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>the sheriff clerk, or</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>the guardian of the person who is under a legal disability.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">Alternatively, the sheriff may decide that the person under a legal disability is capable of managing the money and order that the money is paid directly to that person.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">Where the sheriff orders the money to be paid to the sheriff clerk or a guardian, the sheriff may also tell that person how to manage the money for the benefit of the person under a legal disability.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.9</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can the damages management order be changed?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">An interested person can ask the sheriff to change the damages management order by sending an Application to Change a Damages Management Order to the court and every party.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If a person who has been sent the Application to Change a Damages Management Order objects to the proposed orders, that person must send that Application to Change a Damages Management Order to the court within 10 days of it being sent, setting out that objection.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">After considering the Application to Change a Damages Management Order, and any objection that may have been sent, the sheriff may ...:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>grant the application, and send written orders to the parties and the interested person,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>refuse the application,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>order the parties and the interested person to appear at a discussion in court, where the sheriff will consider whether to make any orders.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.10</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can further instructions about managing the money be given?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">An interested person can also ask the sheriff to tell the sheriff clerk or a guardian how to manage the money by sending an Application for Instructions about a Damages Management Order to the court and every party.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If a guardian is managing the money, the Application for Instructions about a Damages Management Order must also be sent to the guardian.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">If a person who has been sent the Application for Instructions about a Damages Management Order, objects to the proposed instructions, that person must send that Application for Instructions about a Damages Management Order to the court within 10 days of it being sent, with a note setting out that objection.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">After considering the Application for Instructions about a Damages Management Order, and any objection that may have been sent, the sheriff may ...:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>grant the application, and send further instructions to the parties, the interested person and the sheriff clerk or guardian,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>refuse the application,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>order the parties, the interested person and the guardian (if there is one) to appear at a discussion in court, where the sheriff will consider whether to give further instructions.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.11</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>When can someone apply for a child's property administration order?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">A person may ask the sheriff to make a child's property administration order in any simple procedure case where the sheriff has made an order under section 13 of the Children (Scotland) Act 1995 (section 13 is about the payment and management of money to (or for the benefit of) a child).</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.12</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can someone apply for a child's property administration order?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">A person can ask the sheriff to make a child's property administration order by sending an Application for a Child's Property Administration Order to the court and every party.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If a person who has been sent the Application for a Child's Property Administration Order objects to the proposed orders, that person must send that Application for a Child's Property Administration Order to the court within 10 days of it being sent, setting out that objection.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">After considering the Application for a Child's Property Administration Order, and any objection that may have been sent, the sheriff may ...:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>grant the application, and send written orders to the parties and the applicant,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>refuse the application,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>order the parties and the applicant to appear at a discussion in court, where the sheriff will consider whether to make any orders.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>The Equality Act 2010</em></td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.13</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What is an Equality Act 2010 claim?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">An Equality Act 2010 claim is a claim made under section 114(1) of the Equality Act 2010 (section 114 is about claims related to the provision of services, the exercise of public functions, the disposal and management of premises, education (other than in relation to disability), and associations).</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.14</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can the Commission for Equality and Human Rights (“the CEHR”) be notified of an Equality Act 2010 claim?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The claimant must send a copy of the Claim Form in an Equality Act 2010 claim to the CEHR.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.15</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can an Equality Act 2010 claim be transferred to the Employment Tribunal?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff may order an Equality Act 2010 claim to be transferred to the Employment Tribunal.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff must state in that order the reasons for making it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">That order may include an order about expenses.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">When the sheriff makes that order, the sheriff clerk must transmit, within one week of the order, the following things to the Employment Tribunal:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the Claim Form,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>the Response Form,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>any written orders, and</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(d)</td><td>any other document the sheriff orders to be transmitted.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.16</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can an Employment Tribunal case be transferred to simple procedure?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">When proceedings are transferred to simple procedure from the Employment Tribunal under section 140(3) of the Equality Act 2010, the sheriff clerk must register those proceedings as a claim.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff must, within 2 weeks of the claim being registered, order a case management discussion.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>17.17</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What if a question of national security arises in an Equality Act 2010 claim?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">Where the sheriff considers it expedient in the interests of national security, the sheriff may order any of the following persons to be excluded from any or all hearings, case management discussions or discussions in court of an Equality Act 2010 claim:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the claimant,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>the claimant's representative,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>the claimant's courtroom supporter.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">That order may allow an excluded claimant or representative to send a written statement to the court before the case (or part of the case) from which they have been excluded.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">When the sheriff makes an order excluding persons, the sheriff clerk must send a copy of the order to the Advocate General for Scotland.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">Where the sheriff considers it expedient in the interests of national security, the sheriff may take any steps or make any order required to keep secret any or all of the reasons for the sheriff's decision in an Equality Act 2010 claim.</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>

[^key-8d3eb7aa25489db9d21547f1bf7ba28f][^key-a1a9ba980ebbd04425536583f5fa935e][^key-4c18ef1c5301655cce812c77135e559a][^key-68388df939d725b0ed9a84091e7c2cca][^key-21edebca3bf3167a49842eedaa52f93a][^key-115de92b4f991966d939f75753a996aa][^key-f8c0e6b8af402f6cf308d284c1f2f1d1][^key-93cf52466148f29ff47696e63d5b63f8]

### PART 18: Formal service in Scotland

<table>
<tbody>
<tr><th><strong>18.1</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What is this Part about?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">This Part is about how to formally serve a document on someone living in Scotland.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>18.2</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can you formally serve a document on someone who lives in Scotland?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td rowspan="3">(1)</td><td colspan="2">When these Rules require a document to be formally served, the first attempt must be by—</td></tr>
<tr><td>(a)</td><td>a next-day postal service which records delivery, or</td></tr>
<tr><td>(b)</td><td>email, if the person to be formally served with a document has indicated a willingness to accept service by email.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(1A)</td><td colspan="2">Willingness to accept email service of a document may be expressed specifically to the claimant or generally (for example on a website).</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">That may only be done by one of three persons:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the party's solicitor,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>a sheriff officer instructed by the party,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>in respect of next-day postal service which records delivery only, the sheriff clerk (where provided for by rule 6.11(2)).</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">An envelope which contains the document must have the following label written or printed on it:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>THIS ENVELOPE CONTAINS A [NAME OF DOCUMENT] FROM</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>[NAME OF SHERIFF COURT]</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>IF DELIVERY CANNOT BE MADE, THE LETTER MUST BE</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>RETURNED IMMEDIATELY TO THE SHERIFF CLERK AT</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>[FULL ADDRESS OF SHERIFF COURT]</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"></td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">After formally serving a document, a Confirmation of Formal Service must be completed and any evidence of sending or, in the case of email, proof of receipt attached to it (for example, a postal receipt or a copy of an email acknowledgement).</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4A)</td><td colspan="2">Where service is by email, the period of notice runs from the beginning of the day after the date of email service.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">Where a solicitor or sheriff officer has formally served the document, then the Confirmation of Formal Service must be sent to the sheriff court within one week of service taking place.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>18.3</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What if service by post does not work?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">If service by post has not worked, a sheriff officer may formally serve a document in one of three ways:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>delivering it personally,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>leaving it in the hands of a resident at the person's home,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>leaving it in the hands of an employee at the person's place of business.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If none of those ways has worked, the sheriff officer must make diligent inquiries about the person's whereabouts and current residence, and may then formally serve the document in one of two ways:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>depositing it in the person's home or place of business by means of a letter box or other lawful way of doing so, or</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>leaving it at the person's home or place of business in such a way that it is likely to come to the attention of that person.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">If formal service is done in either of those ways, the sheriff officer must also do two more things:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>send a copy of the document to the person by post to the address at which the sheriff officer thinks the person is most likely to be found, and</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>write or print on the envelope containing the document the following label:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>THIS ENVELOPE CONTAINS A [NAME OF DOCUMENT] FROM</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>[NAME OF SHERIFF COURT]</strong></td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>

[^key-1cab1542c3581066872c7f995dbce11c][^key-24a2cb10225508a3878725023c919541][^key-54584bb16b63bf9e8ae73cbafa137ef3][^key-e26838ad2d80f97c65b831553278f4da][^key-7d1ecd4862250b74b8fb52f77541f814][^key-5342c9755fc286a54756538c38d9258c][^key-2264905061fbedf70b131596b1ba09d7]

### PART 19: Formal service outside Scotland

<table>
<tbody>
<tr><th><strong>19.1</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What is this Part about?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">This Part is about how to formally serve a document on someone outside Scotland.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>19.2</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can you formally serve a document on someone who lives outside Scotland?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">Different rules apply depending on the country that the person lives in.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If the person lives in England and Wales, Northern Ireland, the Isle of Man or the Channel Islands, see rule 19.3.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">If the person lives in an EU member state (including Denmark), see rule 19.4.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">If the person lives in a Hague Convention country (other than an EU member state), see rule 19.5.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">If the person lives in a country with which the United Kingdom has a convention about how to serve court documents (such as Algeria, Libya and the United Arab Emirates), see rule 19.6.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">If none of the above applies, see rule 19.7.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>19.3</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can you formally serve a document on someone who lives in England and Wales, Northern Ireland, the Isle of Man or the Channel Islands?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Method</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">There are two ways to formally serve a document on someone who lives in England and Wales, Northern Ireland, the Isle of Man or the Channel Islands.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">It may be done by posting the document to the person's home or business address using a postal service which records delivery. This is called postal service.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">It may also be done by using the rules for personal service under the domestic law of the country where the document is to be served. This is called personal service.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Who can formally serve the document?</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff clerk may formally serve a Claim Form on the respondent by postal service only if:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the claimant is not a company or a partnership, and</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>the claimant is not legally represented.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">Otherwise, postal service may only be done by one of two persons:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the party's solicitor,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>a sheriff officer instructed by the party.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">Personal service may be done by a person who is authorised to do so under the domestic law of the country where the document is to be served.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Additional requirements</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(7)</td><td colspan="2">Where postal service is used, the envelope containing the document must have the following label printed or written on it:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>THIS ENVELOPE CONTAINS A [NAME OF DOCUMENT] FROM</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>[NAME OF SHERIFF COURT], SCOTLAND</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>IF DELIVERY CANNOT BE MADE, THE LETTER MUST BE</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>RETURNED IMMEDIATELY TO THE SHERIFF CLERK AT</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>[FULL ADDRESS OF SHERIFF COURT]</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"></td></tr>
<tr><td>(8)</td><td colspan="2">After formally serving a document, a Confirmation of Formal Service must be completed by the person who served it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(9)</td><td colspan="2">If postal service has been used, any postal receipts must be attached to the Confirmation of Formal Service.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(10)</td><td colspan="2">If a solicitor or a sheriff officer has formally served a document, the Confirmation of Formal Service must be sent to the sheriff court within one week of service taking place.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>19.4</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can you formally serve a document on someone who lives in an EU member state (including Denmark) under the Service Regulation?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Method</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">There are up to 4 ways to formally serve a document on someone who lives in an EU member state (including Denmark) under the Service Regulation, depending on what the law of that member state permits.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">It may be done by posting the document to the person's home or business address using a postal service which records delivery. This is called postal service.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">It may be done by sending the document to a messenger-at-arms and asking them to arrange for it to be served. This is called service by transmitting agency.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">It may be done by sending the document to a person who is entitled to serve court documents in that member state and asking them to arrange for it to be formally served. This is called direct service. This method can only be used if the law of the member state permits it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">It may be done by sending the document to the Secretary of State for Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Affairs and asking the Secretary of State to arrange for it to be formally served by a British consular authority. This is called consular service. This method can always be used if the document is being served on a British national. Otherwise, it can only be used if the law of the member state permits it.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Who can formally serve the document?</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff clerk may formally serve a Claim Form on the respondent by postal service only if:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the claimant is not a company or a partnership, and</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>the claimant is not legally represented.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(7)</td><td colspan="2">Otherwise, postal service may only be done by one of two persons:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the party's solicitor,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>a sheriff officer instructed by the party.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(8)</td><td colspan="2">For the other methods of formal service, the party sends the document to the Secretary of State for Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Affairs or a person who is entitled to serve court documents in the country where the Form or Notice is to be formally served. That person will make the necessary arrangements for formal service.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Additional requirements</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(9)</td><td colspan="2">Where a party chooses service by transmitting agency, the party must give the messenger-at-arms a translation of the document into a language which the recipient understands or an official language of the member state where the document is to be served.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(10)</td><td colspan="2">After translating a document, the translator must complete a Translation Certificate and give it to the party who is formally serving the document.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(11)</td><td colspan="2">Where postal service is used, the envelope containing the document must have the following label printed or written on it:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>THIS ENVELOPE CONTAINS A [NAME OF DOCUMENT] FROM</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>[NAME OF SHERIFF COURT], SCOTLAND</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>IF DELIVERY CANNOT BE MADE, THE LETTER MUST BE</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>RETURNED IMMEDIATELY TO THE SHERIFF CLERK AT</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>[FULL ADDRESS OF SHERIFF COURT]</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"></td></tr>
<tr><td>(12)</td><td colspan="2">That label must also be translated into an official language of the country where the document is to be served, unless English is an official language of that country.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(13)</td><td colspan="2">After formally serving a document by postal service, a Confirmation of Formal Service must be completed by the person who formally served it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(14)</td><td colspan="2">Any postal receipts must be attached to the Confirmation of Formal Service.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(15)</td><td colspan="2">If a solicitor or a sheriff officer has used postal service, the Confirmation of Formal Service must be sent to the sheriff court within one week of formal service taking place.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(16)</td><td colspan="2">If any other method of formal service was used, the party who requested service of the document must send the certificate that the party receives from the person who served the document to the sheriff court within one week of receiving it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(17)</td><td colspan="2">If the document was translated into another language, the Translation Certificate must be sent to the sheriff court with the Confirmation of Formal Service or the certificate from the person who served the document.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>19.5</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can you formally serve a document on someone who lives in a Hague Convention country (other than an EU member state)?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Method</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">There are up to 4 ways to formally serve a document on someone who lives in a Hague Convention country, depending on what the law of that country permits.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">It may be done by posting the document to the person's home or business address using a postal service which records delivery. This is called postal service. This method can only be used if the law of the country permits it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">It may be done by sending the document to the Scottish Ministers and asking them to arrange for it to be formally served. This is called service via central authority. This method can always be used.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">It may be done by sending the document to the Secretary of State for Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Affairs and asking the Secretary of State to arrange for it to be formally served by a British consular authority. This is called consular service. This method can always be used if the document is being formally served on a British national. Otherwise, it can only be used if the law of the country permits it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">It may be done by sending the document to a person who is entitled to serve court documents in that country and asking them to arrange for it to be formally served. This is called service by competent person. This method can only be used if the law of the country permits it.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Who can formally serve the document?</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff clerk may formally serve a Claim Form on the respondent by postal service only if:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the claimant is not a company or a partnership, and</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>the claimant is not legally represented.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(7)</td><td colspan="2">Otherwise, postal service may only be done by one of two persons:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the party's solicitor,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>a sheriff officer instructed by the party.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(8)</td><td colspan="2">For the other methods of formal service, the party sends the Form or Notice to the Scottish Ministers, the Secretary of State for Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Affairs or a person who is entitled to serve court documents in the country where the Form or Notice is to be formally served. That person will make the necessary arrangements for formal service.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Additional requirements</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(9)</td><td colspan="2">Any document must be accompanied by a translation into an official language of the country where it is to be formally served, unless English is an official language of that country.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(10)</td><td colspan="2">After translating a document, the translator must complete a Translation Certificate and give it to the party who is formally serving the Form or Notice.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(11)</td><td colspan="2">Where postal service is used, the envelope containing the document must have the following label printed or written on it:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>THIS ENVELOPE CONTAINS A [NAME OF DOCUMENT] FROM</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>[NAME OF SHERIFF COURT], SCOTLAND</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>IF DELIVERY CANNOT BE MADE, THE LETTER MUST BE</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>RETURNED IMMEDIATELY TO THE SHERIFF CLERK AT</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>[FULL ADDRESS OF SHERIFF COURT]</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"></td></tr>
<tr><td>(12)</td><td colspan="2">That label must also be translated into an official language of the country where the Form or Notice is to be served, unless English is an official language of that country.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(13)</td><td colspan="2">After formally serving a document by postal service, a Confirmation of Formal Service must be completed by the person who served it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(14)</td><td colspan="2">Any postal receipts must be attached to the Confirmation of Formal Service.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(15)</td><td colspan="2">If a solicitor or a sheriff officer has used postal service, the Confirmation of Formal Service must be sent to the sheriff court within one week of service taking place.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(16)</td><td colspan="2">If any other method of formal service was used, the party who requested formal service of the document must send the certificate that the party receives from the person who formally served the document to the sheriff court within one week of receiving it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(17)</td><td colspan="2">If the document was translated into another language, the Translation Certificate must be sent to the sheriff court with the Confirmation of Formal Service or the certificate from the person who served the document.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>19.6</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can you formally serve a document on someone who lives in a country with which the United Kingdom has a convention about how to serve court documents?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Method</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The ways of formally serving a document on someone who lives in a country with which the United Kingdom has a convention about how to serve court documents depends on the convention between the United Kingdom and that country.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">Accordingly, a document can be formally served in any way that is allowed in the convention between the United Kingdom and the country where it is to be served.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Who can formally serve the document?</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">A document can be formally served by a person who is authorised to do so by the convention between the United Kingdom and the country where it is to be served.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Additional requirements</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">Where the convention requires that a document must be accompanied by a translation into an official language of the country where it is to be served, the translator must complete a Translation Certificate and give it to the party who is serving the document.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">The party who requested formal service of the document must send the certificate that the party receives from the person who served the document to the sheriff court within one week of receiving it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">If the document was translated into another language, the Translation Certificate must be sent to the sheriff court with the certificate from the person who served the document.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>19.7</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can you formally serve a document on someone who lives in any other country?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Method</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">There are two ways to formally serve a document on someone who lives in a country where none of the other rules apply.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">It can be done by posting the document to the person's home or business address using a postal service which records delivery. This is called postal service.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">It can also be done by using the rules for personal service under the domestic law of the country where the document is to be served. This is called personal service.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Who can formally serve the document?</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">The sheriff clerk may formally serve a Claim Form on the respondent by postal service only if:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the claimant is not a company or a partnership, and</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>the claimant is not legally represented.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">Otherwise, postal service many only be done by one of two persons:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>the party's solicitor,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>a sheriff officer instructed by the party.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(6)</td><td colspan="2">Personal service may be done by a person who is authorised to do so under the domestic law of the country where the document is to be served.</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="3"><em>Additional requirements</em></td></tr>
<tr><td>(7)</td><td colspan="2">Any document must be accompanied by a translation into an official language of the country where it is to be formally served, unless English is an official language of that country.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(8)</td><td colspan="2">After translating a document, the translator must complete a Translation Certificate and give it to the party who is formally serving the Form or Notice.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(9)</td><td colspan="2">Where postal service is used, the envelope containing the document must have the following label printed or written on it:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>THIS ENVELOPE CONTAINS A [NAME OF DOCUMENT] FROM</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>[NAME OF SHERIFF COURT], SCOTLAND</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>IF DELIVERY CANNOT BE MADE, THE LETTER MUST BE</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>RETURNED IMMEDIATELY TO THE SHERIFF CLERK AT</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"><strong>[FULL ADDRESS OF SHERIFF COURT]</strong></td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td colspan="2"></td></tr>
<tr><td>(10)</td><td colspan="2">That label must also be translated into an official language of the country where the document is to be served, unless English is an official language of that country.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(11)</td><td colspan="2">After formally serving a document by postal service, a Confirmation of Formal Service must be completed by the person who served it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(12)</td><td colspan="2">Any postal receipts must be attached to the Confirmation of Formal Service.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(13)</td><td colspan="2">If a solicitor or a sheriff officer has used postal service, the Confirmation of Formal Service must be sent to the sheriff court within one week of service taking place.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(14)</td><td colspan="2">If any other method of formal service was used, the party who requested formal service of the document must send the certificate that the party receives from the person who formally served the document to the sheriff court within one week of receiving it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(15)</td><td colspan="2">If any other method of formal service was used, the party who requested formal service of the document must also send a Method of Service Abroad Certificate to the sheriff court with the certificate that the party receives from the person who served the document to the sheriff court within one one week of receiving it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(16)</td><td colspan="2">If the document was translated into another language, the Translation Certificate must be sent to the sheriff court with the Confirmation of Formal Service... or the certificate from the person who formally served the document.</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>

[^key-8160306463cd15f07109e25cc8bccb51][^key-c57c69d5b05bb1805669920b37ce7bfb][^key-eb58d9e3ffdffe171b011b4d15a7545f][^key-1c718b785cc89ebb03a237f0e68a7e82][^key-9eca329d5aa3d8be068f543a001d947f][^key-50521c2096c2304bed25af31ad9089ea][^key-849859fc6b3736468bb4a87abe28cfbb][^key-c10cf0cc41c861c9d90088d98f7e2aa0][^key-f4fc38613200e1818550e0450e36863f][^key-dde1433cc430b6d6bf7b9e3add8921ba][^key-d6ae2c979bd534ab8fea91ac0a175954][^key-74dc304486f59a2c595c3f25d203c66f][^key-9059e4bbe2cb40b757e308114f577091][^key-84afcf27133104d07ee43b77a77dccd2][^key-e1fa922f51904d8930a928df2fe30fa5][^key-274bf7f0a899d8b8984aef89b195d7ea][^key-0a3d755f8bf639dce8e888c5fdb9daf0][^key-6afd2576be1003836feb4915c958f154]

### PART 20: Provisional orders

<table>
<tbody>
<tr><th><strong>20.1</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What is this Part about?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">This Part is about provisional orders which protect or secure the claimant's position before the sheriff makes a final decision in a case.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>20.2</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>When can a claimant ask for provisional orders to be made?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The claimant may apply for provisional orders to be made by completing a Provisional Orders Application and sending it to the sheriff court with the Claim Form.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">The claimant may also apply for provisional orders at any time before the sheriff makes a final decision in a case by completing a Provisional Orders Application and sending it to the sheriff court.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">The claimant must also send the Provisional Orders Application to the respondent and any interested person, unless the claimant has asked the court to make the provisional orders without holding a provisional orders hearing.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>20.3</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>What happens when the court receives a Provisional Orders Application?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The next steps depend on whether the claimant has asked the court to grant the Provisional Orders Application with or without holding a hearing.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">If the claimant has asked the court to hold a hearing, before deciding whether to grant the Provisional Orders Application, the sheriff must—</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>send the claimant notice of when and where the hearing is to be held, and</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>order the claimant to tell the respondent and any interested person when and where it is to be held.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">If the claimant has asked the court to grant the Provisional Orders Application without holding a hearing, the sheriff may ...:</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(a)</td><td>grant the Provisional Orders Application and send the claimant written orders containing the provisional orders,</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(b)</td><td>refuse to grant the Provisional Orders Application without holding a hearing and send the claimant notice of when and where the hearing is to be held, or</td></tr>
<tr><td></td><td>(c)</td><td>where the claimant has indicated in Form 20A that they do not want the court to arrange a hearing under paragraph (3)(b), refuse the Provisional Orders Application.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(4)</td><td colspan="2">Where the sheriff grants the Provisional Orders Application without holding a hearing, the sheriff must also fix a provisional orders review hearing and order the claimant to tell the respondent and any interested person when and where it is to be held.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(5)</td><td colspan="2">If the sheriff refuses to grant the Provisional Orders Application without holding a hearing, the sheriff must also order the claimant to send the respondent and any interested person notice of when and where the hearing is to be held.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>20.4</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can the claimant tell the respondent or an interested party about a hearing?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The claimant can tell the respondent or an interested party about any hearing under this Part by sending a Provisional Orders Hearing Notice to the respondent or interested party.</td></tr>
<tr><th><strong>20.5</strong></th><th colspan="2"><strong>How can you ask the court to reconsider provisional orders that it has made?</strong></th></tr>
<tr><td>(1)</td><td colspan="2">The respondent can ask the sheriff to reconsider a provisional order by sending a Provisional Orders Reconsideration Application to the court, the claimant and any interested person.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(2)</td><td colspan="2">An interested person can ask the sheriff to reconsider a provisional order by sending a Provisional Orders Reconsideration Application to the court, the claimant, the respondent and any other interested person.</td></tr>
<tr><td>(3)</td><td colspan="2">When the court receives a Provisional Orders Reconsideration Application, the sheriff must order every person to whom the application was sent to appear at a provisional orders review hearing where the sheriff will consider whether to change the provisional order.</td></tr>
